It's been... gosh, about three years and three months now. It all started right around the time our groundskeeping contract changed our schedule, and those longer days just completely took over my life. We went from doing reasonable hours to being out there from dawn till dusk. They brought in this new management company to oversee the city parks, and the guy running it, Gary, he was a real piece of work. He drove this massive lifted truck, never even got his boots dirty, just drove around pointing at things and yelling about mulch. 

He had us switching out all the nice perennials for these drought-resistant shrubs, which, I mean, I get it, water conservation and all that. But those shrubs are a nightmare to get rooted right in the clay soil we have around here. You gotta dig twice as deep and mix in so much compost just to give them a fighting chance. My buddy Dave on the crew, he actually snapped his favorite spade right in half on that clay. Dave's a good guy, he makes his own venison jerky and brings it in for the guys on Fridays. He uses this hickory smoke marinade that is just out of this world. I tried making it once at home, but I burned the bottom of the pan and it took me three days to scrub it clean... 

Wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, the timeline. Yeah, about three years and three months of feeling like this. It just hasn't lifted since that contract changed.

I really can't. I sit and try to think of something, anything, and... nothing comes to mind. It's like that part of my brain just shut off. It reminds me of this old riding mower we used to have at the depot. A John Deere, one of the older models with the heavy steel deck. The blades on that thing were thick, you could sharpen them a dozen times and they'd still hold an edge. But the ignition switch went bad on it. You'd turn the key and... nothing. Just a dead click. 

We tried everything to fix it. My buddy Hector, he's a wizard with small engines, he took the whole steering column apart. Hector's got this garage at his house that looks like a surgical theater for lawn equipment. Everything is labeled and hung on pegboards. He even has this ultrasonic cleaner for carburetors that he bought at a police auction. He said it belonged to a counterfeiter who used it to clean printing plates or something crazy like that. Hector's always finding these weird deals. He got his wife this massive, antique oak dining table for like fifty bucks from an estate sale, but it was so heavy they had to take the door off the hinges just to get it into their dining room...

Wait, I'm sorry. Did you ask about Hector's mower? I lost my train of thought.

I remember looking at them, these bright orange and white things swimming around, and she was just beaming, practically vibrating with how happy she was. \n\nShe had this whole setup, this massive filtration system that took up half the side yard. It was a beast to install. We had to dig trenches for all the PVC piping, and the ground there was just solid rock. We ended up having to rent a mini excavator, the little yellow one from the rental place down on 4th Street. The guy who runs that place, Sal, he's a trip. Always wearing these loud Hawaiian shirts, even in the dead of winter. He says it keeps his spirit warm. He makes his own hot sauce, too, out of ghost peppers he grows in his greenhouse. He gave me a bottle once, called it \"Sal's Liquid Fire.\" I put one drop on a taco and I swear I couldn't taste anything else for three days. But the ignition switch went bad on it. You'd turn the key and... nothing. Just a dead click. \n\nWe tried everything to fix it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Happiness? Joy? I... no. Not really. Even for a second, it just... it doesn't happen. It's like my battery is completely drained and it won't hold a charge. It makes me think of this old truck my dad used to have, this beat-up Ford F-150. The alternator was shot on it, so you'd have to jump it every single time you wanted to go anywhere. 

We used to take it down to the lake on weekends to go fishing. My dad had this tackle box that was older than I was, covered in rust and old fishing line. He had these hand-painted lures in there that his grandfather made. They were carved out of balsa wood and painted to look like little frogs. He was so proud of those things. He'd never actually use them, just kept them in a special compartment wrapped in cloth. He said balsa wood was too delicate for the kind of bass we were trying to catch. The bass in that lake were monsters, I swear. We used to buy our bait from this little shack near the boat launch. The guy who ran it, old man Henderson, he had a glass eye that never quite looked in the same direction as his good one. He used to tell us these wild stories about seeing a giant catfish that was as big as a Volkswagen Beetle swimming near the dam...

I'm sorry, I did it again, didn't I? What were you asking me? Something about my dad's truck?

The last time I felt pleasure? I honestly... I don't know. I try to reach back and find a memory where I actually felt good, and it's just blank. It's like trying to find a specific tool in the shed when the power's out. You're just fumbling around in the dark. 

Speaking of the shed, we had to do a massive inventory at work yesterday because someone left the main supply lockbox open over the weekend. They think a couple of the commercial leaf blowers walked off. Those things aren't cheap, either. They're the backpack kind, Echo brand, with the heavy-duty straps. The straps on those are actually padded with this memory foam stuff, which is nice because if you're wearing one for six hours straight, your shoulders get raw. My buddy Dave, he actually rigged his up with some extra padding he cut out of an old car seat. Dave's always doing stuff like that. He drives this 1998 Honda Civic that he's basically held together with duct tape and zip ties. He had to replace the radiator on it last month, and he did it right there in the depot parking lot during his lunch break. He spilled coolant all over the asphalt, and the supervisor, Mr. Higgins, lost his mind. Higgins is a stickler for environmental regulations because he got fined by the city a few years back for improper disposal of fertilizer...

Wait... I'm rambling again, aren't I? Did you ask about the leaf blowers? I can't remember what the question was.

Look forward to? No, not really. There's just nothing there. It's just the same thing every day, like a loop. Just wake up, go to work, come home, sleep. It's like watching a movie you've already seen a hundred times, and you know exactly what's going to happen, and none of it's good. 

My wife used to watch this movie, *The Sound of Music*, over and over again. We had it on VHS, one of those double-tape sets because it was so long. The cardboard slipcase was all worn out at the edges. She loved the part where they sing in the gazebo. We actually looked into getting a gazebo for our backyard once. We went to this home and garden show at the convention center downtown. They had all these crazy setups, like full outdoor kitchens with pizza ovens and waterfalls. There was this one guy selling these imported Japanese koi fish. He had this massive tank set up right in the middle of the aisle. The fish were huge, like the size of small dogs, and they were all these brilliant colors—orange, white, black. He said they could live for like fifty years if you took care of them right. You have to monitor the pH levels in the water constantly, though, and they need a special kind of floating pellet food. The pellets are made of fish meal and wheat germ, mostly. I remember reading the ingredients on the bag because it smelled awful, like low tide at the bay...

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.4, F32.5, F32.8, F32.9, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F33.4, F33.5, F33.8, F33.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because... I don't know, most days I wake up and it just feels like there's this heavy, gray weight sitting right on my chest. It's been like that for a long time. Even when I'm out working with the landscaping crew, you know, we're outside, the sun is shining, we're putting in some nice retaining walls—we did this one over on Elm Street last week, beautiful stonework, the homeowner was real picky but it turned out great—anyway, even then, or when I'm around my kids, I just can't shake this low, hollow feeling. It's like I'm just going through the motions. My youngest, he just started middle school and he's having a hard time with math, which is funny because I was terrible at math too, always hated it. But yeah, that heavy feeling, it just doesn't really go away.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, gray weight and the low, hollow feeling?\n\nPatient: It's been... gosh, about three years and three months now. It all started right around the time our groundskeeping contract changed our schedule, and those longer days just completely took over my life. We went from doing reasonable hours to being out there from dawn till dusk. They brought in this new management company to oversee the city parks, and the guy running it, Gary, he was a real piece of work. He drove this massive lifted truck, never even got his boots dirty, just drove around pointing at things and yelling about mulch. \n\nHe had us switching out all the nice perennials for these drought-resistant shrubs, which, I mean, I get it, water conservation and all that. But those shrubs are a nightmare to get rooted right in the clay soil we have around here. You gotta dig twice as deep and mix in so much compost just to give them a fighting chance. My buddy Dave on the crew, he actually snapped his favorite spade right in half on that clay. Dave's a good guy, he makes his own venison jerky and brings it in for the guys on Fridays. He uses this hickory smoke marinade that is just out of this world. I tried making it once at home, but I burned the bottom of the pan and it took me three days to scrub it clean... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, the timeline. Yeah, about three years and three months of feeling like this. It just hasn't lifted since that contract changed.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to enjoy, like working on landscaping projects or spending time with your kids?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. I'm not really sure if I enjoy things anymore or if I'm just... doing them, you know? Like with the kids, my oldest daughter, she just got her learner's permit last month. Which is terrifying, let me tell you. We were out practicing in my old Ford F-150, the green one with the dented tailgate. I've been meaning to pound that dent out for ages. Anyway, the transmission on that truck slips a little when you get up to third gear, so she was getting all frustrated and jerky with the steering wheel. \n\nI bought that truck off a guy named Mike down in the valley a few years back. Mike had this incredible collection of vintage license plates covering his entire garage wall. Every state, going all the way back to the 1930s. He told me he spent twenty years going to swap meets just to find this one specific plate from Alaska. Can you imagine spending twenty years looking for a rusty piece of metal? He had this whole system for cleaning them with fine-grade steel wool and some special polish he ordered all the way from Germany... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Did you ask about the kids driving?\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in anything at all anymore?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? I... I don't really know. Honestly, I'm not sure I could even tell you what that feels like right now. It's just all so flat. I guess maybe... no, not really. It's funny you ask about pleasure, because it makes me think of this one time we were putting in a koi pond for this lady over in the Heights. She was so excited about these fish. She ordered them special from Japan, cost a fortune. I remember looking at them, these bright orange and white things swimming around, and she was just beaming, practically vibrating with how happy she was. \n\nShe had this whole setup, this massive filtration system that took up half the side yard. It was a beast to install. We had to dig trenches for all the PVC piping, and the ground there was just solid rock. We ended up having to rent a mini excavator, the little yellow one from the rental place down on 4th Street. The guy who runs that place, Sal, he's a trip. Always wearing these loud Hawaiian shirts, even in the dead of winter. He says it keeps his spirit warm. He makes his own hot sauce, too, out of ghost peppers he grows in his greenhouse. He gave me a bottle once, called it \"Sal's Liquid Fire.\" I put one drop on a taco and I swear I couldn't taste anything else for three days. My wife was laughing so hard she was crying... \n\nWait, sorry, what was the question? Something about the koi pond?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything that you used to enjoy doing that you still find pleasure in now?\n\nPatient: I really can't. I sit and try to think of something, anything, and... nothing comes to mind. It's like that part of my brain just shut off. It reminds me of this old riding mower we used to have at the depot. A John Deere, one of the older models with the heavy steel deck. The blades on that thing were thick, you could sharpen them a dozen times and they'd still hold an edge. But the ignition switch went bad on it. You'd turn the key and... nothing. Just a dead click. \n\nWe tried everything to fix it. My buddy Hector, he's a wizard with small engines, he took the whole steering column apart. Hector's got this garage at his house that looks like a surgical theater for lawn equipment. Everything is labeled and hung on pegboards. He even has this ultrasonic cleaner for carburetors that he bought at a police auction. He said it belonged to a counterfeiter who used it to clean printing plates or something crazy like that. Hector's always finding these weird deals. He got his wife this massive, antique oak dining table for like fifty bucks from an estate sale, but it was so heavy they had to take the door off the hinges just to get it into their dining room...\n\nWait, I'm sorry. Did you ask about Hector's mower? I lost my train of thought.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel any excitement or look forward to anything?\n\nPatient: Excitement? No. I don't look forward to anything. Waking up just feels like a chore, you know? Like I'm just clocking in for another shift of... nothing. Speaking of shifts, they changed the schedule at the depot last week. Now they want us starting at 5:30 AM instead of 6:00. Half an hour doesn't seem like much, but when you're trying to beat the traffic on I-95, it's brutal. \n\nThe traffic on that stretch is always terrible because of the construction near the overpass. They've been working on that same bridge for three years. I was talking to one of the flaggers there, a guy named Earl. Earl said they keep finding all these old utility lines that aren't on any of the city blueprints. He said they even found an old trolley track buried under the asphalt from like, the 1920s. He collects old railroad spikes, actually. Has buckets of them in his basement. He told me he's trying to weld them together to make a coffee table, but he keeps burning through his welding wire because the iron is so old and full of impurities. He buys his wire in bulk from this supply shop out in the industrial park...\n\nWait, sorry. What did you ask me? Did you ask about my work schedule?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're able to experience happiness or joy, even if it's just for a brief moment?\n\nPatient: Happiness? Joy? I... no. Not really. Even for a second, it just... it doesn't happen. It's like my battery is completely drained and it won't hold a charge. It makes me think of this old truck my dad used to have, this beat-up Ford F-150. The alternator was shot on it, so you'd have to jump it every single time you wanted to go anywhere. \n\nWe used to take it down to the lake on weekends to go fishing. My dad had this tackle box that was older than I was, covered in rust and old fishing line. He had these hand-painted lures in there that his grandfather made. They were carved out of balsa wood and painted to look like little frogs. He was so proud of those things. He'd never actually use them, just kept them in a special compartment wrapped in cloth. He said balsa wood was too delicate for the kind of bass we were trying to catch. The bass in that lake were monsters, I swear. We used to buy our bait from this little shack near the boat launch. The guy who ran it, old man Henderson, he had a glass eye that never quite looked in the same direction as his good one. He used to tell us these wild stories about seeing a giant catfish that was as big as a Volkswagen Beetle swimming near the dam...\n\nI'm sorry, I did it again, didn't I? What were you asking me? Something about my dad's truck?\n\nDoctor: Can you remember the last time you felt any kind of pleasure or enjoyment, no matter how small?\n\nPatient: The last time I felt pleasure? I honestly... I don't know. I try to reach back and find a memory where I actually felt good, and it's just blank. It's like trying to find a specific tool in the shed when the power's out. You're just fumbling around in the dark. \n\nSpeaking of the shed, we had to do a massive inventory at work yesterday because someone left the main supply lockbox open over the weekend. They think a couple of the commercial leaf blowers walked off. Those things aren't cheap, either. They're the backpack kind, Echo brand, with the heavy-duty straps. The straps on those are actually padded with this memory foam stuff, which is nice because if you're wearing one for six hours straight, your shoulders get raw. My buddy Dave, he actually rigged his up with some extra padding he cut out of an old car seat. Dave's always doing stuff like that. He drives this 1998 Honda Civic that he's basically held together with duct tape and zip ties. He had to replace the radiator on it last month, and he did it right there in the depot parking lot during his lunch break. He spilled coolant all over the asphalt, and the supervisor, Mr. Higgins, lost his mind. Higgins is a stickler for environmental regulations because he got fined by the city a few years back for improper disposal of fertilizer...\n\nWait... I'm rambling again, aren't I? Did you ask about the leaf blowers? I can't remember what the question was.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything, no matter how small, that you look forward to on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Look forward to? No, not really. There's just nothing there. It's just the same thing every day, like a loop. Just wake up, go to work, come home, sleep. It's like watching a movie you've already seen a hundred times, and you know exactly what's going to happen, and none of it's good. \n\nMy wife used to watch this movie, *The Sound of Music*, over and over again. We had it on VHS, one of those double-tape sets because it was so long. The cardboard slipcase was all worn out at the edges. She loved the part where they sing in the gazebo. We actually looked into getting a gazebo for our backyard once. We went to this home and garden show at the convention center downtown. They had all these crazy setups, like full outdoor kitchens with pizza ovens and waterfalls. There was this one guy selling these imported Japanese koi fish. He had this massive tank set up right in the middle of the aisle. The fish were huge, like the size of small dogs, and they were all these brilliant colors—orange, white, black. He said they could live for like fifty years if you took care of them right. You have to monitor the pH levels in the water constantly, though, and they need a special kind of floating pellet food. The pellets are made of fish meal and wheat germ, mostly. I remember reading the ingredients on the bag because it smelled awful, like low tide at the bay...\n\nOh, geez. I'm doing it again. Sorry. What was the question? Something about the backyard?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Satisfaction? No. I really can't think of anything. Nothing feels good or satisfying anymore. It's all just... flat. I used to get a little kick out of finishing a big mowing job, you know, looking back at the straight lines in the grass. But now it's just dirt and noise.

It all started right around the time our groundskeeping contract changed our schedule, and those longer days just completely took over my life. We went from doing reasonable hours to being out there from dawn till dusk. They brought in this new management company to oversee the city parks, and the guy running it, Gary, he was a real piece of work. He drove this massive lifted truck, never even got his boots dirty, just drove around pointing at things and yelling about mulch. \n\nHe had us switching out all the nice perennials for these drought-resistant shrubs, which, I mean, I get it, water conservation and all that. But those shrubs are a nightmare to get rooted right in the clay soil we have around here. You gotta dig twice as deep and mix in so much compost just to give them a fighting chance. My buddy Dave on the crew, he actually snapped his favorite spade right in half on that clay. Dave's a good guy, he makes his own venison jerky and brings it in for the guys on Fridays. He uses this hickory smoke marinade that is just out of this world. I tried making it once at home, but I burned the bottom of the pan and it took me three days to scrub it clean... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, the timeline. Yeah, about three years and three months of feeling like this. It just hasn't lifted since that contract changed.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to enjoy, like working on landscaping projects or spending time with your kids?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. I'm not really sure if I enjoy things anymore or if I'm just... doing them, you know? Like with the kids, my oldest daughter, she just got her learner's permit last month. Which is terrifying, let me tell you. We were out practicing in my old Ford F-150, the green one with the dented tailgate. I've been meaning to pound that dent out for ages. Anyway, the transmission on that truck slips a little when you get up to third gear, so she was getting all frustrated and jerky with the steering wheel. \n\nI bought that truck off a guy named Mike down in the valley a few years back. Mike had this incredible collection of vintage license plates covering his entire garage wall. Every state, going all the way back to the 1930s. He told me he spent twenty years going to swap meets just to find this one specific plate from Alaska. Can you imagine spending twenty years looking for a rusty piece of metal? He had this whole system for cleaning them with fine-grade steel wool and some special polish he ordered all the way from Germany... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Did you ask about the kids driving?\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in anything at all anymore?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? I... I don't really know. Honestly, I'm not sure I could even tell you what that feels like right now. It's just all so flat. I guess maybe... no, not really. It's funny you ask about pleasure, because it makes me think of this one time we were putting in a koi pond for this lady over in the Heights. She was so excited about these fish. She ordered them special from Japan, cost a fortune. I remember looking at them, these bright orange and white things swimming around, and she was just beaming, practically vibrating with how happy she was. \n\nShe had this whole setup, this massive filtration system that took up half the side yard. It was a beast to install. We had to dig trenches for all the PVC piping, and the ground there was just solid rock. We ended up having to rent a mini excavator, the little yellow one from the rental place down on 4th Street. The guy who runs that place, Sal, he's a trip. Always wearing these loud Hawaiian shirts, even in the dead of winter. He says it keeps his spirit warm. He makes his own hot sauce, too, out of ghost peppers he grows in his greenhouse. He gave me a bottle once, called it \"Sal's Liquid Fire.\" I put one drop on a taco and I swear I couldn't taste anything else for three days. My wife was laughing so hard she was crying... \n\nWait, sorry, what was the question? Something about the koi pond?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything that you used to enjoy doing that you still find pleasure in now?\n\nPatient: I really can't. I sit and try to think of something, anything, and... nothing comes to mind. It's like that part of my brain just shut off. It reminds me of this old riding mower we used to have at the depot. A John Deere, one of the older models with the heavy steel deck. The blades on that thing were thick, you could sharpen them a dozen times and they'd still hold an edge. But the ignition switch went bad on it. You'd turn the key and... nothing. Just a dead click. \n\nWe tried everything to fix it. My buddy Hector, he's a wizard with small engines, he took the whole steering column apart. Hector's got this garage at his house that looks like a surgical theater for lawn equipment. Everything is labeled and hung on pegboards. He even has this ultrasonic cleaner for carburetors that he bought at a police auction. He said it belonged to a counterfeiter who used it to clean printing plates or something crazy like that. Hector's always finding these weird deals. He got his wife this massive, antique oak dining table for like fifty bucks from an estate sale, but it was so heavy they had to take the door off the hinges just to get it into their dining room...\n\nWait, I'm sorry. Did you ask about Hector's mower? I lost my train of thought.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel any excitement or look forward to anything?\n\nPatient: Excitement? No. I don't look forward to anything. Waking up just feels like a chore, you know? Like I'm just clocking in for another shift of... nothing. Speaking of shifts, they changed the schedule at the depot last week. Now they want us starting at 5:30 AM instead of 6:00. Half an hour doesn't seem like much, but when you're trying to beat the traffic on I-95, it's brutal. \n\nThe traffic on that stretch is always terrible because of the construction near the overpass. They've been working on that same bridge for three years. I was talking to one of the flaggers there, a guy named Earl. Earl said they keep finding all these old utility lines that aren't on any of the city blueprints. He said they even found an old trolley track buried under the asphalt from like, the 1920s. He collects old railroad spikes, actually. Has buckets of them in his basement. He told me he's trying to weld them together to make a coffee table, but he keeps burning through his welding wire because the iron is so old and full of impurities. He buys his wire in bulk from this supply shop out in the industrial park...\n\nWait, sorry. What did you ask me? Did you ask about my work schedule?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're able to experience happiness or joy, even if it's just for a brief moment?\n\nPatient: Happiness? Joy? I... no. Not really. Even for a second, it just... it doesn't happen. It's like my battery is completely drained and it won't hold a charge. It makes me think of this old truck my dad used to have, this beat-up Ford F-150. The alternator was shot on it, so you'd have to jump it every single time you wanted to go anywhere. \n\nWe used to take it down to the lake on weekends to go fishing. My dad had this tackle box that was older than I was, covered in rust and old fishing line. He had these hand-painted lures in there that his grandfather made. They were carved out of balsa wood and painted to look like little frogs. He was so proud of those things. He'd never actually use them, just kept them in a special compartment wrapped in cloth. He said balsa wood was too delicate for the kind of bass we were trying to catch. The bass in that lake were monsters, I swear. We used to buy our bait from this little shack near the boat launch. The guy who ran it, old man Henderson, he had a glass eye that never quite looked in the same direction as his good one. He used to tell us these wild stories about seeing a giant catfish that was as big as a Volkswagen Beetle swimming near the dam...\n\nI'm sorry, I did it again, didn't I? What were you asking me? Something about my dad's truck?\n\nDoctor: Can you remember the last time you felt any kind of pleasure or enjoyment, no matter how small?\n\nPatient: The last time I felt pleasure? I honestly... I don't know. I try to reach back and find a memory where I actually felt good, and it's just blank. It's like trying to find a specific tool in the shed when the power's out. You're just fumbling around in the dark. \n\nSpeaking of the shed, we had to do a massive inventory at work yesterday because someone left the main supply lockbox open over the weekend. They think a couple of the commercial leaf blowers walked off. Those things aren't cheap, either. They're the backpack kind, Echo brand, with the heavy-duty straps. The straps on those are actually padded with this memory foam stuff, which is nice because if you're wearing one for six hours straight, your shoulders get raw. My buddy Dave, he actually rigged his up with some extra padding he cut out of an old car seat. Dave's always doing stuff like that. He drives this 1998 Honda Civic that he's basically held together with duct tape and zip ties. He had to replace the radiator on it last month, and he did it right there in the depot parking lot during his lunch break. He spilled coolant all over the asphalt, and the supervisor, Mr. Higgins, lost his mind. Higgins is a stickler for environmental regulations because he got fined by the city a few years back for improper disposal of fertilizer...\n\nWait... I'm rambling again, aren't I? There's just nothing there. It's just the same thing every day, like a loop. Just wake up, go to work, come home, sleep. It's like watching a movie you've already seen a hundred times, and you know exactly what's going to happen, and none of it's good. \n\nMy wife used to watch this movie, *The Sound of Music*, over and over again. We had it on VHS, one of those double-tape sets because it was so long. The cardboard slipcase was all worn out at the edges. She loved the part where they sing in the gazebo. We actually looked into getting a gazebo for our backyard once. We went to this home and garden show at the convention center downtown. They had all these crazy setups, like full outdoor kitchens with pizza ovens and waterfalls. There was this one guy selling these imported Japanese koi fish. He had this massive tank set up right in the middle of the aisle. The fish were huge, like the size of small dogs, and they were all these brilliant colors—orange, white, black. He said they could live for like fifty years if you took care of them right. You have to monitor the pH levels in the water constantly, though, and they need a special kind of floating pellet food. Pleased or content? I... I really don't think so. I can't remember feeling pleased about anything lately. It's like my brain just doesn't register that feeling anymore. You could hand me a winning lottery ticket and I'd probably just stare at it. 

I actually used to buy scratch-offs every Friday after work at this little Gulf station near my house. He keeps a jar of those little non-dairy creamer cups next to it, the ones that are basically just corn syrup and oil. I read an article once about how those things are terrible for your cholesterol. My doctor's been harping on my cholesterol for years. He put me on statins, but they made my joints ache, especially my knees. My left knee has been bad ever since I fell off the tailgate of a pickup truck back in '99. We were loading up a bunch of retaining wall blocks—those heavy, interlocking concrete ones that weigh like eighty pounds each. They have this rough, textured face that completely shreds your gloves. I go through a pair of leather work gloves every three weeks because of those things. I usually buy the Wells Lamont ones from Home Depot, the ones with the cinched wrist so the dirt doesn't get inside...

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.4, F32.5, F32.8, F32.9, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F33.4, F33.5, F33.8, F33.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because... I don't know, most days I wake up and it just feels like there's this heavy, gray weight sitting right on my chest. It's been like that for a long time. Even when I'm out working with the landscaping crew, you know, we're outside, the sun is shining, we're putting in some nice retaining walls—we did this one over on Elm Street last week, beautiful stonework, the homeowner was real picky but it turned out great—anyway, even then, or when I'm around my kids, I just can't shake this low, hollow feeling. It's like I'm just going through the motions. My youngest, he just started middle school and he's having a hard time with math, which is funny because I was terrible at math too, always hated it. But yeah, that heavy feeling, it just doesn't really go away.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, gray weight and the low, hollow feeling?\n\nPatient: It's been... gosh, about three years and three months now. It all started right around the time our groundskeeping contract changed our schedule, and those longer days just completely took over my life. We went from doing reasonable hours to being out there from dawn till dusk. They brought in this new management company to oversee the city parks, and the guy running it, Gary, he was a real piece of work. He drove this massive lifted truck, never even got his boots dirty, just drove around pointing at things and yelling about mulch. \n\nHe had us switching out all the nice perennials for these drought-resistant shrubs, which, I mean, I get it, water conservation and all that. But those shrubs are a nightmare to get rooted right in the clay soil we have around here. You gotta dig twice as deep and mix in so much compost just to give them a fighting chance. My buddy Dave on the crew, he actually snapped his favorite spade right in half on that clay. Dave's a good guy, he makes his own venison jerky and brings it in for the guys on Fridays. He uses this hickory smoke marinade that is just out of this world. I tried making it once at home, but I burned the bottom of the pan and it took me three days to scrub it clean... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, the timeline. Yeah, about three years and three months of feeling like this. It just hasn't lifted since that contract changed.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to enjoy, like working on landscaping projects or spending time with your kids?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. I'm not really sure if I enjoy things anymore or if I'm just... doing them, you know? Like with the kids, my oldest daughter, she just got her learner's permit last month. Which is terrifying, let me tell you. We were out practicing in my old Ford F-150, the green one with the dented tailgate. I've been meaning to pound that dent out for ages. Anyway, the transmission on that truck slips a little when you get up to third gear, so she was getting all frustrated and jerky with the steering wheel. \n\nI bought that truck off a guy named Mike down in the valley a few years back. Mike had this incredible collection of vintage license plates covering his entire garage wall. Every state, going all the way back to the 1930s. He told me he spent twenty years going to swap meets just to find this one specific plate from Alaska. Can you imagine spending twenty years looking for a rusty piece of metal? He had this whole system for cleaning them with fine-grade steel wool and some special polish he ordered all the way from Germany... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Did you ask about the kids driving?\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in anything at all anymore?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? I... I don't really know. Honestly, I'm not sure I could even tell you what that feels like right now. It's just all so flat. I guess maybe... no, not really. It's funny you ask about pleasure, because it makes me think of this one time we were putting in a koi pond for this lady over in the Heights. She was so excited about these fish. She ordered them special from Japan, cost a fortune. I remember looking at them, these bright orange and white things swimming around, and she was just beaming, practically vibrating with how happy she was. \n\nShe had this whole setup, this massive filtration system that took up half the side yard. It was a beast to install. We had to dig trenches for all the PVC piping, and the ground there was just solid rock. We ended up having to rent a mini excavator, the little yellow one from the rental place down on 4th Street. The guy who runs that place, Sal, he's a trip. Always wearing these loud Hawaiian shirts, even in the dead of winter. He says it keeps his spirit warm. He makes his own hot sauce, too, out of ghost peppers he grows in his greenhouse. He gave me a bottle once, called it \"Sal's Liquid Fire.\" I put one drop on a taco and I swear I couldn't taste anything else for three days. My wife was laughing so hard she was crying... \n\nWait, sorry, what was the question? Something about the koi pond?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything that you used to enjoy doing that you still find pleasure in now?\n\nPatient: I really can't. I sit and try to think of something, anything, and... nothing comes to mind. It's like that part of my brain just shut off. It reminds me of this old riding mower we used to have at the depot. A John Deere, one of the older models with the heavy steel deck. The blades on that thing were thick, you could sharpen them a dozen times and they'd still hold an edge. But the ignition switch went bad on it. You'd turn the key and... nothing. Just a dead click. \n\nWe tried everything to fix it. My buddy Hector, he's a wizard with small engines, he took the whole steering column apart. Hector's got this garage at his house that looks like a surgical theater for lawn equipment. Everything is labeled and hung on pegboards. He even has this ultrasonic cleaner for carburetors that he bought at a police auction. He said it belonged to a counterfeiter who used it to clean printing plates or something crazy like that. Hector's always finding these weird deals. He got his wife this massive, antique oak dining table for like fifty bucks from an estate sale, but it was so heavy they had to take the door off the hinges just to get it into their dining room...\n\nWait, I'm sorry. Did you ask about Hector's mower? I lost my train of thought.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel any excitement or look forward to anything?\n\nPatient: Excitement? No. I don't look forward to anything. Waking up just feels like a chore, you know? Like I'm just clocking in for another shift of... nothing. Speaking of shifts, they changed the schedule at the depot last week. Now they want us starting at 5:30 AM instead of 6:00. Half an hour doesn't seem like much, but when you're trying to beat the traffic on I-95, it's brutal. \n\nThe traffic on that stretch is always terrible because of the construction near the overpass. They've been working on that same bridge for three years. I was talking to one of the flaggers there, a guy named Earl. Earl said they keep finding all these old utility lines that aren't on any of the city blueprints. He said they even found an old trolley track buried under the asphalt from like, the 1920s. He collects old railroad spikes, actually. Has buckets of them in his basement. He told me he's trying to weld them together to make a coffee table, but he keeps burning through his welding wire because the iron is so old and full of impurities. He buys his wire in bulk from this supply shop out in the industrial park...\n\nWait, sorry. What did you ask me? Did you ask about my work schedule?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're able to experience happiness or joy, even if it's just for a brief moment?\n\nPatient: Happiness? Joy? I... no. Not really. Even for a second, it just... it doesn't happen. It's like my battery is completely drained and it won't hold a charge. It makes me think of this old truck my dad used to have, this beat-up Ford F-150. The alternator was shot on it, so you'd have to jump it every single time you wanted to go anywhere. \n\nWe used to take it down to the lake on weekends to go fishing. My dad had this tackle box that was older than I was, covered in rust and old fishing line. He had these hand-painted lures in there that his grandfather made. They were carved out of balsa wood and painted to look like little frogs. He was so proud of those things. He'd never actually use them, just kept them in a special compartment wrapped in cloth. He said balsa wood was too delicate for the kind of bass we were trying to catch. The bass in that lake were monsters, I swear. We used to buy our bait from this little shack near the boat launch. The guy who ran it, old man Henderson, he had a glass eye that never quite looked in the same direction as his good one. He used to tell us these wild stories about seeing a giant catfish that was as big as a Volkswagen Beetle swimming near the dam...\n\nI'm sorry, I did it again, didn't I? What were you asking me? Something about my dad's truck?\n\nDoctor: Can you remember the last time you felt any kind of pleasure or enjoyment, no matter how small?\n\nPatient: The last time I felt pleasure? I honestly... I don't know. I try to reach back and find a memory where I actually felt good, and it's just blank. It's like trying to find a specific tool in the shed when the power's out. You're just fumbling around in the dark. \n\nSpeaking of the shed, we had to do a massive inventory at work yesterday because someone left the main supply lockbox open over the weekend. They think a couple of the commercial leaf blowers walked off. Those things aren't cheap, either. They're the backpack kind, Echo brand, with the heavy-duty straps. The straps on those are actually padded with this memory foam stuff, which is nice because if you're wearing one for six hours straight, your shoulders get raw. My buddy Dave, he actually rigged his up with some extra padding he cut out of an old car seat. Dave's always doing stuff like that. He drives this 1998 Honda Civic that he's basically held together with duct tape and zip ties. He had to replace the radiator on it last month, and he did it right there in the depot parking lot during his lunch break. He spilled coolant all over the asphalt, and the supervisor, Mr. Higgins, lost his mind. Higgins is a stickler for environmental regulations because he got fined by the city a few years back for improper disposal of fertilizer...\n\nWait... I'm rambling again, aren't I? Did you ask about the leaf blowers? I can't remember what the question was.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything, no matter how small, that you look forward to on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Look forward to? No, not really. There's just nothing there. It's just the same thing every day, like a loop. Just wake up, go to work, come home, sleep. It's like watching a movie you've already seen a hundred times, and you know exactly what's going to happen, and none of it's good. \n\nMy wife used to watch this movie, *The Sound of Music*, over and over again. We had it on VHS, one of those double-tape sets because it was so long. The cardboard slipcase was all worn out at the edges. She loved the part where they sing in the gazebo. We actually looked into getting a gazebo for our backyard once. We went to this home and garden show at the convention center downtown. They had all these crazy setups, like full outdoor kitchens with pizza ovens and waterfalls. There was this one guy selling these imported Japanese koi fish. He had this massive tank set up right in the middle of the aisle. The fish were huge, like the size of small dogs, and they were all these brilliant colors—orange, white, black. He said they could live for like fifty years if you took care of them right. You have to monitor the pH levels in the water constantly, though, and they need a special kind of floating pellet food. The pellets are made of fish meal and wheat germ, mostly. I remember reading the ingredients on the bag because it smelled awful, like low tide at the bay...\n\nOh, geez. I'm doing it again. Sorry. What was the question? Something about the backyard?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activity, no matter how small or insignificant it may seem, that you still find slightly enjoyable or that brings you even a tiny bit of satisfaction?\n\nPatient: Satisfaction? No. I really can't think of anything. Nothing feels good or satisfying anymore. It's all just... flat. I used to get a little kick out of finishing a big mowing job, you know, looking back at the straight lines in the grass. But now it's just dirt and noise. \n\nSpeaking of dirt, we had to lay down about thirty yards of topsoil over at the new corporate park on Route 9 yesterday. The dump truck backed up way too far and completely crushed the concrete curb. The driver, this guy named Sal, he just got out and started screaming at the dispatcher on his radio. Sal's radio is one of those old Motorola two-ways with the heavy curly cord. It reminds me of the CB radio my uncle had in his camper van when I was a kid. It was a 1978 Dodge Tradesman, painted this awful puke green with orange shag carpet on the walls and ceiling. He drove that thing all the way to Mount Rushmore back in '85. He brought me back this little snow globe with the presidents' heads in it, but the water inside got all cloudy and yellow after a few years. I think the seal went bad on the bottom. It had this little rubber stopper that dried out and cracked, probably because I used to keep it on the windowsill right above the radiator in my childhood bedroom. That radiator used to clank so loud in the winter, it sounded like someone hitting a pipe with a wrench...\n\nI'm sorry. I completely lost the thread again. What did you ask me? Something about the corporate park?\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any recent activity where you felt even slightly pleased or content with the outcome?\n\nPatient: Pleased or content? I... I really don't think so. I can't remember feeling pleased about anything lately. It's like my brain just doesn't register that feeling anymore. You could hand me a winning lottery ticket and I'd probably just stare at it. \n\nI actually used to buy scratch-offs every Friday after work at this little Gulf station near my house. The guy who runs it, Sanjay, he always has a fresh pot of coffee going, even at five in the afternoon. It's that really strong, burnt-smelling kind that stains the bottom of the glass pot. He keeps a jar of those little non-dairy creamer cups next to it, the ones that are basically just corn syrup and oil. I read an article once about how those things are terrible for your cholesterol. My doctor's been harping on my cholesterol for years. He put me on statins, but they made my joints ache, especially my knees. My left knee has been bad ever since I fell off the tailgate of a pickup truck back in '99. We were loading up a bunch of retaining wall blocks—those heavy, interlocking concrete ones that weigh like eighty pounds each. They have this rough, textured face that completely shreds your gloves. I go through a pair of leather work gloves every three weeks because of those things. I usually buy the Wells Lamont ones from Home Depot, the ones with the cinched wrist so the dirt doesn't get inside...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm drifting off again, aren't I? Did you ask about the lottery tickets? I forgot what we were talking about.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Look forward to? No. I don't really care about food anymore, and a shower is just... another chore. Just another thing I have to drag myself through before I can go back to sleep. I guess I don't look forward to anything at all.

Used to enjoy? I... I don't know. I honestly can't think of a single thing. It's been so long since I actually liked doing something, my mind is just totally blank. 

I guess I used to go down to Miller's Pond sometimes, just to sit on the bank. But they ruined that place. The town let all this thick green algae take over the water. My crew actually got contracted to clean up the edges there a couple years ago. We had to walk the perimeter wearing these heavy backpack sprayers to put down algaecide. The straps on the sprayer they gave me were completely shot, just frayed to hell, and they dug right into my collarbone all afternoon. I tried to fix it by wrapping it in duct tape, but my boss only buys that cheap dollar-store tape, not the real 3M stuff. The adhesive on the cheap kind just turns into this sticky gray goo as soon as the sun hits it. I left a roll of it on the dashboard of my old Ford Ranger once, and it basically melted right into the plastic. Getting it off was a nightmare, I had to use half a bottle of Goo Gone and a plastic putty knife. That Goo Gone stuff smells exactly like oranges, you know? Like those little hard orange candies they give you with the bill at the Chinese buffet down on Main Street. The owner there, Mr. Chen, he always keeps a giant glass bowl of them right next to the cash register. The register is one of those antique brass ones, really heavy, with the little bell that dings every time the drawer pops open...

Wait. I'm sorry, my brain just completely wandered off again. What were we talking about? Did you ask me about the Chinese place?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Slightly enjoyable now? No. I mean, nothing. I just... I don't get any enjoyment out of anything anymore. It's all just flat. 

I used to like mowing the lawn, actually. Just riding the mower around the yard, smelling the cut grass. But my mower's been broken since last summer. It's a John Deere, one of those zero-turn ones with the green and yellow paint. The paint on the deck is starting to chip off, though, and it's getting all rusty underneath. Rust is the worst. We had this old wheelbarrow at work that got so rusty the bottom just completely fell out of it while I was hauling a load of river rock. River rock is so heavy, you know? We were using it to build this dry creek bed for a client over in the Oakwood subdivision. They had this massive oak tree in the front yard that dropped acorns everywhere. Thousands of them. You couldn't even walk across the driveway without twisting your ankle on one. The squirrels loved it, though. There was this one squirrel with a chunk missing from its ear, like it had been in a fight or something. It used to sit on the fence post and just chatter at us while we worked. Sounded like a little machine gun going off...

Sorry. I'm doing it again. My mind just won't stay put. Did you ask me about the mower?

The adhesive on the cheap kind just turns into this sticky gray goo as soon as the sun hits it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Pleasure? No. I can't... I can't think of a single thing. Nothing feels good anymore. It's just... nothing. 

I guess I used to like sitting on the porch with a cup of coffee in the morning, before the sun came all the way up. But my coffee maker broke a few weeks ago. It was one of those fancy ones that grinds the beans right before it brews. My sister got it for me for my birthday a couple years back. She always buys these really expensive gifts, like she's trying to prove something. Last year she got me this thick wool sweater from Ireland. It's so itchy I can't even wear it. I tried washing it with a ton of fabric softener, but it didn't help. The fabric softener was that lavender scent, you know? Supposed to be relaxing. My wife used to buy the lavender laundry detergent all the time. She'd wash the bedsheets in it, and the whole bedroom would smell like a flower shop for days. We bought this huge, heavy wooden bed frame at an antique store up in the mountains once. It took three guys to carry it up the stairs to our room. One of the guys accidentally scraped the wall with the headboard, left this long white mark right down the hallway paint. The paint color was called "Sea Glass," kind of a pale green. We spent a whole weekend painting that hallway, listening to old classic rock on this little portable radio I had. The antenna on that radio was bent, so you had to hold it at a weird angle to get the station to come in clear...

Wait. I'm sorry. I lost track again. What did you ask me? Something about the coffee maker?

The motor on it was so loud, it sounded like a hair dryer running all night. It drove the neighbor's dog crazy. It was a golden retriever, I think, named Buster. Buster used to dig these massive holes along the fence line trying to get into our yard. We had to put down chicken wire and cover it with mulch to stop him. The mulch was that dyed black kind—it looks nice for about two weeks, and then it just fades to this dull gray color if it rains too much. And man, it rained so much that spring, the whole yard turned into a swamp. I remember my boots getting sucked right off my feet in the mud...\n\nOh, shoot. I did it again, didn't I? Something about the hot water heater?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about something you used to enjoy doing that you haven't done in a while?\n\nPatient: Used to enjoy? I... I don't know. We had this old wheelbarrow at work that got so rusty the bottom just completely fell out of it while I was hauling a load of river rock. River rock is so heavy, you know? We were using it to build this dry creek bed for a client over in the Oakwood subdivision. They had this massive oak tree in the front yard that dropped acorns everywhere. Thousands of them. You couldn't even walk across the driveway without twisting your ankle on one. The squirrels loved it, though. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Life events? I... I don't know if you'd call it an event, exactly. But things really started getting bad about three years and three months ago. That's when the groundskeeping contract changed, and they completely messed up our schedule. The longer days just totally took over my life, you know? We were working from sunup to sundown, trying to keep up with all the new properties they added. One of the new places was this huge corporate park out by the highway. The grass there grew so fast, I swear you could watch it get taller. We had to use these massive commercial mowers, the ones with the huge diesel engines. Diesel exhaust always gives me a headache if I smell it too long. It reminds me of when I was a kid and my dad used to drive this old Peterbilt semi-truck. He hauled lumber mostly, big stacks of pine boards that smelled like sap. Sometimes he'd let me ride in the cab with him, and we'd listen to the CB radio. The truckers all had these crazy handles, like "Rubber Duck" and "Snowman." I wanted my handle to be "Silver Bullet," because I had this shiny silver bicycle... 

Wait. I'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? My brain just keeps drifting off. What did you ask me about? The new contract?

Pleasure in the last few days? No. No, nothing at all. I just wake up and it's another day to get through. It's all the same heavy feeling. 

I did watch some TV on Tuesday night, I think it was. Just staring at the screen, really. It was one of those home renovation shows where they knock down all the walls to make an open concept. Pleasure? No... I don't think so. I really can't think of anything. It's just... flat. Everything is just flat. 

I guess I noticed the birds outside the window this morning, just for a second. There was this one cardinal, bright red, sitting on the telephone wire. The pellets are made of fish meal and wheat germ, mostly. I remember reading the ingredients on the bag because it smelled awful, like low tide at the bay...\n\nOh, geez. I'm doing it again. Sorry. What was the question? Something about the backyard?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activity, no matter how small or insignificant it may seem, that you still find slightly enjoyable or that brings you even a tiny bit of satisfaction?\n\nPatient: Satisfaction? No. I really can't think of anything. Nothing feels good or satisfying anymore. It's all just... flat. I used to get a little kick out of finishing a big mowing job, you know, looking back at the straight lines in the grass. But now it's just dirt and noise. \n\nSpeaking of dirt, we had to lay down about thirty yards of topsoil over at the new corporate park on Route 9 yesterday. The dump truck backed up way too far and completely crushed the concrete curb. The driver, this guy named Sal, he just got out and started screaming at the dispatcher on his radio. Sal's radio is one of those old Motorola two-ways with the heavy curly cord. It reminds me of the CB radio my uncle had in his camper van when I was a kid. It was a 1978 Dodge Tradesman, painted this awful puke green with orange shag carpet on the walls and ceiling. He drove that thing all the way to Mount Rushmore back in '85. He brought me back this little snow globe with the presidents' heads in it, but the water inside got all cloudy and yellow after a few years. I think the seal went bad on the bottom. It had this little rubber stopper that dried out and cracked, probably because I used to keep it on the windowsill right above the radiator in my childhood bedroom. That radiator used to clank so loud in the winter, it sounded like someone hitting a pipe with a wrench...\n\nI'm sorry. I completely lost the thread again. What did you ask me? Something about the corporate park?\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any recent activity where you felt even slightly pleased or content with the outcome?\n\nPatient: Pleased or content? I... I really don't think so. I can't remember feeling pleased about anything lately. It's like my brain just doesn't register that feeling anymore. You could hand me a winning lottery ticket and I'd probably just stare at it. \n\nI actually used to buy scratch-offs every Friday after work at this little Gulf station near my house. The guy who runs it, Sanjay, he always has a fresh pot of coffee going, even at five in the afternoon. It's that really strong, burnt-smelling kind that stains the bottom of the glass pot. He keeps a jar of those little non-dairy creamer cups next to it, the ones that are basically just corn syrup and oil. I read an article once about how those things are terrible for your cholesterol. My doctor's been harping on my cholesterol for years. He put me on statins, but they made my joints ache, especially my knees. My left knee has been bad ever since I fell off the tailgate of a pickup truck back in '99. We were loading up a bunch of retaining wall blocks—those heavy, interlocking concrete ones that weigh like eighty pounds each. They have this rough, textured face that completely shreds your gloves. I go through a pair of leather work gloves every three weeks because of those things. I usually buy the Wells Lamont ones from Home Depot, the ones with the cinched wrist so the dirt doesn't get inside...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm drifting off again, aren't I? Did you ask about the lottery tickets? I forgot what we were talking about.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any small thing that you might look forward to, even if it's just something as simple as a favorite meal or a warm shower?\n\nPatient: Look forward to? No. I don't really care about food anymore, and a shower is just... another chore. Just another thing I have to drag myself through before I can go back to sleep. I guess I don't look forward to anything at all. \n\nSpeaking of showers, though, the hot water heater at our place has been acting up. It's this huge old tank, probably from the eighties, sitting down in the basement. The basement always smells a little damp, you know? Like wet cardboard. We used to store all our holiday decorations down there in these big plastic bins with the snap-on lids. The red ones were for Christmas, the orange ones for Halloween. My wife bought this massive inflatable pumpkin one year for the front yard. It had a little fan built into the base that had to run constantly to keep it inflated. The motor on it was so loud, it sounded like a hair dryer running all night. It drove the neighbor's dog crazy. It was a golden retriever, I think, named Buster. Buster used to dig these massive holes along the fence line trying to get into our yard. We had to put down chicken wire and cover it with mulch to stop him. The mulch was that dyed black kind—it looks nice for about two weeks, and then it just fades to this dull gray color if it rains too much. And man, it rained so much that spring, the whole yard turned into a swamp. I remember my boots getting sucked right off my feet in the mud...\n\nOh, shoot. I did it again, didn't I? I just start talking and my brain just wanders off. What did you ask me? Something about the hot water heater?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about something you used to enjoy doing that you haven't done in a while?\n\nPatient: Used to enjoy? I... I don't know. I honestly can't think of a single thing. It's been so long since I actually liked doing something, my mind is just totally blank. \n\nI guess I used to go down to Miller's Pond sometimes, just to sit on the bank. But they ruined that place. The town let all this thick green algae take over the water. My crew actually got contracted to clean up the edges there a couple years ago. We had to walk the perimeter wearing these heavy backpack sprayers to put down algaecide. The straps on the sprayer they gave me were completely shot, just frayed to hell, and they dug right into my collarbone all afternoon. I tried to fix it by wrapping it in duct tape, but my boss only buys that cheap dollar-store tape, not the real 3M stuff. The adhesive on the cheap kind just turns into this sticky gray goo as soon as the sun hits it. I left a roll of it on the dashboard of my old Ford Ranger once, and it basically melted right into the plastic. Getting it off was a nightmare, I had to use half a bottle of Goo Gone and a plastic putty knife. That Goo Gone stuff smells exactly like oranges, you know? Like those little hard orange candies they give you with the bill at the Chinese buffet down on Main Street. The owner there, Mr. Chen, he always keeps a giant glass bowl of them right next to the cash register. The register is one of those antique brass ones, really heavy, with the little bell that dings every time the drawer pops open...\n\nWait. I'm sorry, my brain just completely wandered off again. What were we talking about? Did you ask me about the Chinese place?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything that you used to enjoy doing that you still find slightly enjoyable now?\n\nPatient: Slightly enjoyable now? No. I mean, nothing. I just... I don't get any enjoyment out of anything anymore. It's all just flat. \n\nI used to like mowing the lawn, actually. Just riding the mower around the yard, smelling the cut grass. But my mower's been broken since last summer. It's a John Deere, one of those zero-turn ones with the green and yellow paint. The paint on the deck is starting to chip off, though, and it's getting all rusty underneath. Rust is the worst. We had this old wheelbarrow at work that got so rusty the bottom just completely fell out of it while I was hauling a load of river rock. River rock is so heavy, you know? We were using it to build this dry creek bed for a client over in the Oakwood subdivision. They had this massive oak tree in the front yard that dropped acorns everywhere. Thousands of them. You couldn't even walk across the driveway without twisting your ankle on one. The squirrels loved it, though. There was this one squirrel with a chunk missing from its ear, like it had been in a fight or something. It used to sit on the fence post and just chatter at us while we worked. Sounded like a little machine gun going off...\n\nSorry. I'm doing it again. My mind just won't stay put. Did you ask me about the mower?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything that gives you a sense of pleasure or enjoyment?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No. I can't... I can't think of a single thing. Nothing feels good anymore. It's just... nothing. \n\nI guess I used to like sitting on the porch with a cup of coffee in the morning, before the sun came all the way up. But my coffee maker broke a few weeks ago. It was one of those fancy ones that grinds the beans right before it brews. My sister got it for me for my birthday a couple years back. She always buys these really expensive gifts, like she's trying to prove something. Last year she got me this thick wool sweater from Ireland. It's so itchy I can't even wear it. I tried washing it with a ton of fabric softener, but it didn't help. The fabric softener was that lavender scent, you know? Supposed to be relaxing. My wife used to buy the lavender laundry detergent all the time. She'd wash the bedsheets in it, and the whole bedroom would smell like a flower shop for days. We bought this huge, heavy wooden bed frame at an antique store up in the mountains once. It took three guys to carry it up the stairs to our room. One of the guys accidentally scraped the wall with the headboard, left this long white mark right down the hallway paint. The paint color was called \"Sea Glass,\" kind of a pale green. We spent a whole weekend painting that hallway, listening to old classic rock on this little portable radio I had. The antenna on that radio was bent, so you had to hold it at a weird angle to get the station to come in clear...\n\nWait. I'm sorry. I lost track again. What did you ask me? Something about the coffee maker?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant life events or changes in the past few years that you think might be related to how you're feeling now?\n\nPatient: Life events? I... I don't know if you'd call it an event, exactly. But things really started getting bad about three years and three months ago. That's when the groundskeeping contract changed, and they completely messed up our schedule. The longer days just totally took over my life, you know? We were working from sunup to sundown, trying to keep up with all the new properties they added. One of the new places was this huge corporate park out by the highway. The grass there grew so fast, I swear you could watch it get taller. We had to use these massive commercial mowers, the ones with the huge diesel engines. Diesel exhaust always gives me a headache if I smell it too long. It reminds me of when I was a kid and my dad used to drive this old Peterbilt semi-truck. He hauled lumber mostly, big stacks of pine boards that smelled like sap. Sometimes he'd let me ride in the cab with him, and we'd listen to the CB radio. The truckers all had these crazy handles, like \"Rubber Duck\" and \"Snowman.\" I wanted my handle to be \"Silver Bullet,\" because I had this shiny silver bicycle... \n\nWait. I'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? My brain just keeps drifting off. What did you ask me about? The new contract?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything that has given you pleasure or enjoyment in the last few days?\n\nPatient: Pleasure in the last few days? No. No, nothing at all. I just wake up and it's another day to get through. It's all the same heavy feeling. \n\nI did watch some TV on Tuesday night, I think it was. Just staring at the screen, really. It was one of those home renovation shows where they knock down all the walls to make an open concept. I never understood why people want open concept. It just means you can hear the dishwasher running while you're trying to watch TV. Our dishwasher makes this awful grinding noise during the rinse cycle. I tried taking the bottom rack out to look at the filter, but the little plastic wheels on the rack are all brittle. One of them snapped right off in my hand. It rolled under the refrigerator, and when I got down on my hands and knees to fish it out with a wire hanger, I found a dog toy under there. It was a little green rubber bone. We haven't had a dog in five years. Buster was a good boy, a golden retriever mix. He used to shed like crazy, though. We had this vacuum cleaner that was supposed to be specially designed for pet hair, but the brush roll would just get tangled up constantly. You had to sit there with a pair of scissors and cut all the hair out of it every time you vacuumed. I always used these orange-handled scissors that we kept in the junk drawer. That drawer is so full of random stuff—dead batteries, dried-up pens, rubber bands...\n\nI'm sorry. I'm rambling again, aren't I? My head is just... it won't stay on track. Did you ask me about the TV show?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything that gives you a sense of pleasure or enjoyment?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No... I don't think so. I really can't think of anything. It's just... flat. Everything is just flat. \n\nI guess I noticed the birds outside the window this morning, just for a second. There was this one cardinal, bright red, sitting on the telephone wire. We used to have a bird feeder out back, one of those wooden ones shaped like a little house. My grandfather built it for me when I was a teenager. He was always in his woodshop, making things out of scrap cedar. His whole garage smelled like cedar sawdust and old motor oil. He had this vintage Ford Mustang he was always tinkering with, cherry red. He'd spend hours polishing the chrome bumpers until you could see your reflection in them. I tried to help him once, but I used the wrong kind of rag and left all these tiny scratches on the metal. He didn't get mad, though. He just went into the house and brought out two bottles of root beer. They were the kind in the thick glass bottles, where you need a bottle opener to get the cap off. We had this heavy metal bottle opener shaped like a bear's head mounted on the wall in the kitchen. My mom hated it, said it was tacky, but my dad refused to take it down. He bought it at a flea market on a road trip we took to the Grand Canyon when I was ten. We drove the whole way in this beat-up station wagon with fake wood paneling on the sides. The air conditioning was broken, so we had to roll the windows down, and the wind would just whip through the car...\n\nWait... I'm doing it again. I'm sorry. My mind just keeps wandering off. What did you ask me? Something about the birds?"
